## Overview

The Luminal Memory codebase is an advanced framework designed to solve the fundamental limitation of Large Language Models (LLMs): finite context windows. At its core, it manages persistent, infinite conversational memory by treating every interaction as a structured `Node` that contributes to a continuous `Chain`. Incoming data is processed through specialized extensions—such as document ingestion or web search tools—and stored in a managed knowledge base. When an LLM prompt is issued, the system's retrieval engine uses sophisticated algorithms (like BM25 and TF-IDF) to query this memory, fetching only the most relevant historical context nodes before passing them to the `ConversationManager` for final synthesis. This architecture ensures that the AI maintains deep, long-term coherence while remaining modular enough to integrate various external tools and data sources.

## `agents/ARCHITECTURE.md`

**638 lines** | 0 functions/classes

**Purpose:**
Luminal Memory is a sophisticated, zero-dependency JavaScript library designed to provide Large Language Models (LLMs) with persistent, infinite conversational memory without requiring external models or significant GPU resources. Its primary goal is to manage the entire state and history of an LLM conversation, allowing the stateless model to appear context-aware over long interactions.

**What It Does:**
The library manages conversation history as a linear chain of data nodes in RAM. On every turn, it executes a complex memory pipeline:

1.  **Context Management:** It first appends the new user message and subsequently the LLM's response to the internal node chain.
2.  **Sliding Window:** It maintains a limited "sliding window" (the most recent $N$ nodes) that is injected directly into the prompt for immediate context.
3.  **Advanced Retrieval:** Before sending the request, it performs an algorithmic search:
    *   It uses a **Bloom Filter** to quickly check if deep historical retrieval is necessary.
    *   If needed, it calculates relevance using **TF-IDF** and **BM25 scoring** against the entire history.
    *   The top relevant historical nodes are retrieved and injected into the prompt alongside the sliding window context.
4.  **Memory Optimization (Compaction):** To prevent memory overflow, the system periodically "compresses" or archives older segments of the conversation. These archived blocks are summarized, stored in cold storage (like IndexedDB), and represented by special marker nodes that retain key topics and vectors for future retrieval.
5.  **Resource Control:** It actively monitors RAM usage against a defined limit, warning or blocking operations when memory thresholds are reached.

**Role in the Project:**
Luminal Memory acts as the central **state machine and context provider** for any LLM application. It abstracts away the complexity of managing long-term conversational state, allowing developers to treat the LLM endpoint as if it were inherently stateful, while the library handles all the underlying data persistence, retrieval logic, and memory optimization required for continuous, high-volume use.

</details>

## `agents/atomic-coding-engine.md`

**271 lines** | 0 functions/classes

This document describes the **Atomic Coding Engine**, a highly structured pipeline designed to automatically convert a natural language request (e.g., "build X") into fully functional, tested, and documented code.

**Purpose:**
The engine's primary purpose is to eliminate ambiguity and manual decision-making during software development by strictly separating system control from content generation. It ensures that the Large Language Model (LLM) acts only as a specialized content filler, while the surrounding system handles all architectural decisions, file management, testing, and iteration logic.

**Functionality:**
The process is broken down into six deterministic phases:

1.  **Specification:** The system gathers context (web search, memory, config) to generate a detailed specification skeleton. The LLM then fills this skeleton with defined content blocks (inputs, outputs, constraints).
2.  **File Scaffolding:** The system deterministically creates the necessary file structure and empty files (e.g., `index.js`, `index.test.js`) using predefined section markers (`// === IMPORTS ===`).
3.  **Code Fill:** The engine iterates through each section marker, generating code block by block via targeted prompts. This ensures that every piece of code is generated in isolation and assembled sequentially into the correct file structure.
4.  **Test Generation:** A test scaffold is created first, defining required test cases based on the specification. The LLM then fills these specific test blocks to verify expected behaviors.
5.  **Validation (The Core Loop):** The system executes all generated tests and performs syntax checks. If a test fails, the system programmatically identifies the failure, feeds the error message back to the relevant code block, and prompts the LLM for a targeted fix—without asking the LLM *why* it failed. This loop continues up to three times per failing block.
6.  **Documentation:** Finally, the engine creates a documentation scaffold (e.g., `README.md`) and uses the LLM to fill specific sections with usage examples, API descriptions, and configuration details.

**Role in the Project:**
The Atomic Coding Engine serves as the project's core automation layer. It transforms an open-ended prompt into a reliable, repeatable software development lifecycle (SDLC) pipeline. Its role is to enforce structure, manage state, control iteration, and guarantee that all generated code is tested against its own specifications before being considered complete.

</details>

## `agents/atomic-research-engine.md`

**492 lines** | 0 functions/classes

The Atomic Research Engine design document outlines a structured, automated pipeline designed to convert abstract research concepts into fully executed, verifiable projects.

**Purpose:**
Its primary purpose is to provide a deterministic framework for AI agents to systematically execute complex research tasks. It treats the resulting documentation not as the goal itself, but as proof of the work completed through rigorous, phased execution.

**Functionality (What it Does):**
The engine adapts the ten required sections of a federal grant proposal (like NASA SBIR) into a multi-phase agent pipeline. This process guides the system through:
1. **Problem Definition:** Identifying gaps and limitations using web search and memory recall.
2. **Objective Tree Generation:** Programmatically defining specific, measurable sub-objectives.
3. **Prior Art Survey:** Conducting targeted searches to map existing research and identify reusable components.
4. **Work Plan Creation:** Decomposing the entire project into a hierarchical task list where every single action is defined as an "atomic executable action."

**Role in the Project:**
The engine serves as the core operational methodology for the project. It transforms high-level user input ("research X") into a structured, actionable workflow. By forcing the research through these distinct phases—from defining the problem to creating granular tasks and documenting prior art—it ensures comprehensive coverage, minimizes scope creep, and guarantees that all executed work is traceable back to a defined objective.

</details>

## `agents/brief_v1.md`

**84 lines** | 0 functions/classes

This document outlines the architecture for **Luminal Memory**, a system designed to provide local Large Language Models (LLMs) with persistent, infinite conversational context without requiring additional computational resources or dedicated models.

**Purpose and Role:**
The primary role of Luminal Memory is to solve the problem of limited LLM context windows by creating a lightweight, model-free history management layer. It allows small, resource-constrained LLMs (e.g., 3B parameters) to maintain memory across unlimited conversation lengths while adhering strictly to hardware-first constraints and avoiding any additional neural network inference.

**What the System Does:**
The system manages conversational history through a multi-stage process:

1.  **Context Management:** It maintains an active "Sliding Window" for immediate context, which moves forward with each turn. Older nodes are systematically moved into an archive layer.
2.  **Compaction and Archiving:** When memory usage increases, the system performs "Trim & Zip," compressing large blocks of history onto disk archives. Instead of losing access to this data, it inserts a **Compaction Node** that summarizes the archived range, giving the LLM macro-awareness of the historical context without loading it into active memory.
3.  **Zero-Model Retrieval:** When a user query references old information outside the active window, the system executes a sophisticated, purely algorithmic retrieval pipeline:
    *   It first uses **BM25** for keyword searching within the active nodes.
    *   For archived history, it employs ultra-fast filters (Bloom Filters) and semantic ranking using **TF-IDF vectors**.
    *   This process identifies candidate archive blocks, decompresses only the necessary data, and runs a final search to pinpoint exact historical nodes.
4.  **Injection:** The retrieved relevant nodes are then injected back into the LLM's active sliding window for the model to use in its response.

In essence, Luminal Memory acts as an intelligent, disk-backed memory cache that uses mathematical algorithms (BM25, Bloom Filters, TF-IDF) rather than neural networks to retrieve and inject historical context seamlessly.

</details>

## `agents/docgen-redesign-notes.md`

**162 lines** | 0 functions/classes

This document outlines the comprehensive specifications for an exhaustive, multi-tiered codebase documentation tool designed to process any project type or language.

**Purpose:**
The primary purpose is to generate highly detailed, structured technical documentation that covers every file in a repository, regardless of its format (source code, styles, configuration, etc.). It aims to create a complete record of the entire codebase structure and content for maintainability and knowledge transfer.

**What it Does:**
1.  **Exhaustive Documentation:** It mandates documenting *all* file types—including JS/TS, CSS, HTML, Markdown, JSON, YAML, TOML, etc.—by extracting specific contextual information (e.g., key selectors for CSS, schema definitions for config files, page structure for HTML).
2.  **Three-Tier File Handling:** It categorizes all project files into three tiers:
    *   **Tier 1 (Document Fully):** All source code and documentation are fully analyzed and summarized.
    *   **Tier 2 (Acknowledge Only):** Development environment folders (`.vscode`, `.idea`) are noted with a brief description of their presence, but their contents are ignored.
    *   **Tier 3 (Skip Entirely):** Build outputs, dependencies (`node_modules`), and binary files are completely excluded from documentation.
3.  **Structured Output:** The final output must be highly structured:
    *   **Table of Contents (TOC):** Files must be grouped using a nested, collapsible `<details>` structure that accurately mirrors the physical folder hierarchy, rather than being a flat list.
    *   **Content Formatting:** Specific rules govern how content is summarized for different file types (e.g., CSS summaries focus on selectors and tokens; config files document schemas).

**Role in the Project:**
This specification serves as the definitive guide for implementing a universal documentation generator. It dictates the scope, depth, structural formatting, and exclusion criteria necessary to transform an entire codebase into a single, navigable, and comprehensive technical reference manual.

</details>

## `agents/GEMINI_NOTES.md`

**147 lines** | 4 functions/classes

This source file implements a specialized tool designed to generate highly rigorous and formally structured technical research proposals and feasibility studies.

**Purpose:**
The primary purpose is to elevate the quality of generated research output by preventing the system from producing shallow or unstructured summaries. Instead, it forces the research process into an explicit, deterministic framework modeled after strict federal grant standards (specifically NASA/NSF SBIR guidelines).

**Functionality:**
1.  **Blueprint Definition:** It first defines a fixed blueprint (`NASA_PROPOSAL_SECTIONS`) consisting of mandatory sections (e.g., Executive Summary, State of the Art, Technical Methodology, Work Plan, Data Management).
2.  **Structured Execution:** The core logic iterates through this defined sequence of sections. For each section, it constructs a targeted query and executes an information-gathering process, ensuring that research is conducted specifically for that module's requirements.
3.  **Compilation:** Finally, it compiles the gathered content into a single, formal Markdown document, maintaining the mandated structure and professional formatting of a real grant proposal.

**Role in the Project:**
This tool acts as an advanced, specialized extension that provides deep structural integrity to research generation. By adopting the rigid architecture of federal grants, it ensures that any output is not merely a summary but a comprehensive, multi-section technical document suitable for formal academic or industrial review. It serves as the definitive mechanism for generating official-grade, deeply structured research artifacts.

### Exports

- `executeNasaResearchProposal` (function)
- `createNasaProposalTool` (function)
- `NASA_PROPOSAL_SECTIONS` (const)

### Functions & Methods

#### `executeNasaResearchProposal(topic, outputDir = './proposals')` — function, line 55

* **Purpose:** Generates a comprehensive, structured technical research proposal document in Markdown format, simulating adherence to federal grant standards for a given topic.
* **Inputs:**
    * `topic` (string): The central subject matter of the research proposal.
    * `outputDir` (string, optional): The directory path where the final proposal file will be saved. Defaults to `./proposals`.
* **Outputs:** A Promise that resolves upon successful completion of writing the Markdown file. The primary output is a new `.md` file written to the specified `outputDir`.
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * This function performs multiple asynchronous operations, including simulated intelligence gathering (`gatherSectionIntelligence`) and file system writes.
    * It ensures the target output directory exists using `fs.mkdir({ recursive: true })`.
    * The generated filename is deterministic, based on a sanitized version of the input topic and the current date (e.g., `topic-nasa-proposal-YYYY-MM-DD.md`).
    * Progress updates are logged to the console during section drafting.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
export async function executeNasaResearchProposal(topic, outputDir = './proposals') {
  console.log(`[NASA-Proposal] Initializing federal grant research pipeline for: "${topic}"`);

  let proposalSectionsContent = [];

  // THE LOOP: Iterate through the official grant sections deterministically
  for (const section of NASA_PROPOSAL_SECTIONS) {
    console.log(`[NASA-Proposal] Drafting section: ${section.title}`);

    // Construct a targeted query vector based on the grant section requirements
    const sectionQuery = `${topic} ${section.key} ${section.description}`;
    
    // Simulate or execute targeted information gathering for this specific section
    // (In your system, you hook your web search or memory retrieval here)
    const sectionData = await gatherSectionIntelligence(sectionQuery, section.key);

    proposalSectionsContent.push({
      title: section.title,
      content: sectionData
    });
  }

  // Compile into the final official-grade proposal document
  const timestamp = new Date().toISOString().split('T')[0];
  const safeFilename = topic.toLowerCase().replace(/[^a-z0-9]+/g, '-') + `-NASA-Proposal-${timestamp}.md`;
  const targetPath = path.join(outputDir, safeFilename);

  let finalMarkdown = `# TECHNICAL RESEARCH PROPOSAL & FEASIBILITY STUDY\n`;
  finalMarkdown += `*Prepared via Luminal Memory Autonomous Engine (NASA/NSF Standardized Blueprint)*\n`;
  finalMarkdown += `*Target Subject: ${topic}* | *Date: ${new Date().toLocaleDateString()}*\n\n`;
  finalMarkdown += `---\n\n`;

  proposalSectionsContent.forEach(sec => {
    finalMarkdown += `## ${sec.title}\n\n`;
    finalMarkdown += `${sec.content}\n\n`;
    finalMarkdown += `---\n\n`;
  });

  await fs.mkdir(outputDir, { recursive: true });
  await fs.writeFile(targetPath, finalMarkdown, 'utf8');
  // ... (truncated)
```

#### `createNasaProposalTool(config = {})` — function, line 119

* **Purpose:** Creates and returns a specialized tool designed to generate formal, multi-section technical research proposals modeled after strict federal grant standards (e.g., NASA/NSF SBIR).
* **Inputs:**
    * `config`: Object (Optional) - Configuration object for the tool. Can contain:
        * `outputDir`: String - The directory where the generated proposal files should be saved. Defaults to `'./proposals'`.
* **Outputs:** A configured Tool instance that, when executed, generates and returns the research proposal content/result.
* **Behavioral Notes:** When the returned tool is used, it requires a `topic` string parameter which defines the core subject of the proposal. The execution relies on an external function (`executeNasaResearchProposal`) to perform the actual generation and file saving.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
export function createNasaProposalTool(config = {}) {
  const outputDir = config.outputDir || './proposals';

  return new Tool({
    name: "nasa_research_proposal",
    description: "Generate a formal, multi-section technical research proposal and feasibility study modeled after strict federal grant standards (NASA/NSF SBIR guidelines). Includes executive summary, state-of-the-art background, technical methodology, work plans, and data management plans. Use when the user requests an official, deeply structured research document or grant-style proposal.",
    parameters: {
      type: "object",
      properties: {
        topic: {
          type: "string",
          description: "The core technology, system, or research subject for the proposal."
        }
      },
      required: ["topic"]
    },
    execute: async function (params) {
      const topic = params.topic;
      if (!topic) throw new Error("Topic is required for the research proposal.");

      const result = await executeNasaResearchProposal(topic, outputDir);
      return result;
    }
  });
}
```

## `agents/ledger.md`

**171 lines** | 1 functions/classes

This document serves as the definitive **Research Ledger** for the "Luminal Memory" system, acting as a comprehensive technical specification and architectural blueprint. Its primary purpose is to consolidate all research findings, resolve ambiguities, and lock down the Minimum Viable Product (MVP) scope for Version 1.

**Functionality:**
The ledger details a sophisticated memory architecture designed to manage long-term conversational context efficiently. Key functional components include:

*   **Memory Structure:** A linear node chain utilizing a fixed-size sliding window as active working memory.
*   **Retrieval System:** It employs **BM25** (rather than raw TF-IDF) for primary keyword retrieval, which first searches the active window and only escalates to archived data if confidence is low. The system avoids complex embedding models entirely, relying on algorithmic methods.
*   **Storage Strategy:** A hybrid approach using **SQLite** for metadata and indexing, paired with flat, compressed (**zstd**) files for node content. Bloom filters are used as non-critical pre-filtering accelerators.
*   **Maintenance/Compaction:** The system automatically "trims" or compacts old nodes to disk, generating structured, LLM-generated summaries (in JSON format) at the boundaries of archived blocks to prevent context loss.

**Role in Project:**
This ledger dictates the technical constraints and scope for development. By locking down consensus points across multiple rounds of expert input, it establishes a clear, non-negotiable roadmap for v1: linear structure only, single-user/single-session access, and specific algorithmic choices (e.g., BM25 tuning parameters). It explicitly defines what is *out* of scope for the initial release (e.g., branching, concurrency) while providing a detailed blueprint for implementation.

### Functions & Methods

#### `managing()` — class, line 122

* **Purpose:** Manages a linear, chained knowledge base by tracking nodes, summarizing content into compressed blocks, and facilitating advanced retrieval using hybrid search algorithms.
* **Inputs:**
    * `Active Window Nodes`: List of the last N nodes used for immediate context and BM25 searching.
    * `Target Node Range`: The specific segment of nodes to be summarized and archived during compaction.
    * `Query`: The user or system query used to initiate retrieval processes (e.g., BM25 search).
* **Outputs:**
    * `Compaction Node`: A structured, compressed block containing the summary of the processed node range, inserted into the SQLite chain.
    * `Updated State`: Updated Bloom filter and TF-IDF vector representing the expanded knowledge base.
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * **Token Management:** Continuously monitors context saturation and tracks token count per node to manage a processing budget.
    * **Compaction Process (Trim & Zip):** When triggered, it extracts a target node range, calls an LLM for structured JSON summarization, serializes the summary using zstd compression, inserts a Compaction Node into SQLite, updates the Bloom filter with archived terms, and computes a TF-IDF vector for the block.
    * **Retrieval Loop (Hybrid):** Retrieval is multi-staged: it first runs BM25 on the active window; if confidence is low, it uses the bloom filter to gate search against archive blocks; finally, it uses TF-IDF cosine similarity to rank candidates, decompressing and injecting relevant nodes into the current context.
    * **State Mutation:** The class maintains state by appending new Compaction Nodes to an SQLite chain and updating internal indices (Bloom Filter, TF-IDF vector). Temporary nodes are flushed after each turn completes.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
   - Python class managing linear chain (parent_id linking)
   - Slices active window of last N nodes for prompt injection
   - Tracks token count per node for budget calculations

3. **Trim & Zip Engine**
   - Monitors context saturation / token count
   - Extracts target node range
   - Calls running LLM for structured JSON summary
   - Serializes to zstd-compressed flat file
   - Inserts Compaction Node into SQLite chain
   - Populates bloom filter with terms from archived nodes
   - Computes TF-IDF vector for the block

4. **Hybrid Retrieval Loop**
   - BM25 on active window (always, every query)
   - If confidence low → bloom filter gate on archive blocks
   - TF-IDF cosine similarity to rank candidate blocks
   - Decompress top 1-2 blocks → BM25 within → inject relevant nodes
   - Flush temporary nodes after turn completes
```

## `agents/luminal-memory-reference.md`

**1359 lines** | 4 functions/classes

**Purpose:** Luminal Memory is a zero-dependency JavaScript library designed to provide infinite conversational memory for Large Language Models (LLMs). Its primary purpose is to overcome the fundamental limitation of LLM context windows, which cause historical data loss in long conversations.

**What It Does:**
The library implements a sophisticated, layered memory architecture that manages conversation history across three distinct levels:

1.  **Sliding Window:** Maintains a limited, highly recent set of turns for immediate inclusion in the prompt.
2.  **Retrieval Buffer (Active Search):** When context is needed but not in the window, it uses pure mathematical methods—specifically **BM25** and **TF-IDF** ranking—to search the history and retrieve only the most relevant past information.
3.  **Cold Storage (Archival):** Older conversation turns are compressed and stored in IndexedDB. Retrieval from this archive is gated by a **Bloom Filter** for efficiency, ensuring that only highly probable blocks of history are decompressed and ranked before being injected into the prompt.

Furthermore, it strictly manages token budgets across system prompts, recall context, and the sliding window to ensure no generated prompt ever exceeds the model's maximum context limit. It also supports advanced features like tool discovery and self-critique loops.

**Role in the Project:**
Luminal Memory serves as the core state management and intelligence layer for any LLM application requiring long-term conversational coherence. Its role is to act as a comprehensive memory pipeline, abstracting away the complexity of context window limitations by handling storage (IndexedDB), retrieval (BM25/TF-IDF), and prompt assembly (Sliding Window/Budgeting) entirely within pure JavaScript, making it model-agnostic and highly portable across browser and Node.js environments.

#### `validateTension(tension, originalQuery)` — function, line 843

* **Purpose:** Validates if a given tension string meets minimum length requirements and shares at least one meaningful word overlap with an original query.
* **Inputs:**
    * `tension` (string): The text being validated; must be at least 15 characters long.
    * `originalQuery` (string): The source text used to check for semantic overlap.
* **Outputs:** boolean: Returns `true` if the tension is sufficiently long and overlaps with the query, otherwise returns `false`.
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * Validation requires two conditions to pass: 1) The `tension` string must be at least 15 characters long; and 2) At least one word (4+ characters) from the `tension` must exist in the `originalQuery`.
    * Comparison is case-insensitive.
    * Overlap checks only consider words of four or more characters.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
function validateTension(tension, originalQuery) {
  // Minimum length check
  if (!tension || tension.length < 15) return false;

  // Entity overlap: tension must reference at least one meaningful word
  // (4+ characters) from the original query
  const queryWords = new Set(
    originalQuery.toLowerCase().split(/\W+/).filter(w => w.length >= 4)
  );
  const tensionWords = tension.toLowerCase().split(/\W+/).filter(w => w.length >= 4);
  const overlap = tensionWords.filter(w => queryWords.has(w));
  return overlap.length >= 1;
}
```

</details>

</details>

## `agents/NOTES.md`

**143 lines** | 4 functions/classes

This source file serves as a comprehensive **technical status report and roadmap** for "Luminal Memory," an advanced conversational AI system designed for deep, context-aware interaction and tool utilization. Its primary purpose is to document the current state of its complex architecture, detail known limitations, and outline future development goals.

### Core Functionality (What It Does)
The system manages conversation history through a sophisticated memory engine that combines:
1.  **Context Management:** Uses linear node chains, sliding windows, and recall buffers to maintain context. It supports deep retrieval by querying an indexed archive of past interactions using vector embeddings for complex, multi-hop questions.
2.  **Tool Integration:** Implements a dynamic tool system where external functions are discovered via search (BM25) and injected into the prompt when needed. This allows the AI to interact with APIs and web services in a structured manner.
3.  **User Interface:** Features an elaborate, real-time demo UI that visually represents all internal processes—from node creation and recall effects to selection modes for memory trimming and branching.

### Project Role and Current Status
The document details the system's current maturity by listing both working features and critical known issues. The immediate role of the project is focused on stabilizing core components, specifically:
*   Improving **LLM reliability** in determining when a tool is needed (intent detection).
*   Implementing robust **persistence** for the active chat state across page refreshes.
*   Enhancing the **tool system** to handle caching and ensure search results are properly stored as recallable nodes.

### Future Development Roadmap
The file outlines two major, non-blocking future features:
1.  **Automatic Model Configuration:** Implementing a feature that automatically detects and configures the system's chat template (e.g., thinking delimiters, tool call formats) by querying the underlying LLM server, ensuring compatibility with any model family without manual setup.
2.  **Extension Builder Tool:** A powerful new capability allowing users to deterministically scaffold, fill, and compile entirely new custom tools (extensions). This process follows a structured, atomic pipeline that guides the user through defining the tool's structure, gathering necessary APIs, filling the logic, and compiling it into a functional code file.

### Functions & Methods

#### `with()` — class, line 19

* **Purpose:** Provides an advanced, agentic chat interface for knowledge retrieval and interaction by dynamically selecting and executing specialized tools based on user queries.
* **Inputs:**
    * `Query`: The user's text input (string).
    * `Context/Memory`: Historical conversation data and retrieved nodes from the LuminalMemory engine (system message injection).
    * `Tool Description`: A set of available tool descriptions used for BM25 matching.
* **Outputs:**
    * `Response`: The final generated answer, rendered with Markdown support.
    * `Sources`: Clickable orange hyperlinks citing external sources or retrieved memory nodes.
    * `Nodes`: Structured data representing the conversation history and knowledge graph elements (stored in LuminalMemory).
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * **Tool Execution:** Tools are lazy-loaded; they are only injected into the context if BM25 matching between the query and tool description exceeds a low threshold (0.1).
    * **LLM Interaction:** The system uses one-shot examples to guide the LLM in outputting structured JSON or function-call syntax for tool decision making.
    * **UI/UX:** Features include an atomic CSS design, GIF-masked ASCII background animation, and a selection mode allowing users to define trim/branch points (START/END markers).
    * **Known Issues (Critical):**
        * LLM intent detection is unreliable; the model may answer instead of routing or generate poor search queries.
        * The low tool threshold (0.1) causes over-matching, requiring better discrimination or explicit LLM confirmation that a tool is needed.
        * Tool results are not currently stored as persistent nodes in the chain, limiting future recall capabilities.
        * Persistence issues exist: the active chat chain resets on page refresh and requires IndexedDB implementation for state saving.
    * **Side Effects:** Successful tool execution injects results into the prompt as a system message before the user's query.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
- Base Tool class with name, description, parameters, execute, toSchema (includes instruction template)
- ToolRegistry with BM25-based discovery (threshold: 0.1)
- Tools are lazy-loaded into context — only injected when BM25 matches query to tool description
- Web Search tool using Firecrawl keyless endpoint (free, no API key)
- Tool decision uses one-shot example to get Gemma to output JSON
- Parsed formats: JSON, Gemma tool_call syntax, function-call style
- Results injected as system message before user's query in the prompt
- Sources rendered as clickable orange hyperlinks after response

### Demo UI
- Atomic CSS design (atoms → molecules → organisms → templates)
- GIF-masked ASCII background animation (any bg.gif auto-scales)
- Morphing geometric shapes (polygon interpolation)
- Wireframe grid with dot-matrix icons and orange accent stars
- Chat panels with wireframe headers ([ QUERY ], [ RESPONSE ], NODE #)
- User panels left-aligned, assistant right-aligned
- Window boundary divider (orange line, label in whitespace)
- Sidebar stats in left whitespace (nodes, window, memory, archives, status)
- Selection mode for trim/branch (click panels, orange outline, ✂ START/END markers)
- Right-side context info panel during selection
- Confirmation modals for destructive actions
- Recall FX — ghost nodes flicker in right whitespace on recall
- Markdown rendering for assistant responses (marked.js)
- Source citations as clickable links after tool-assisted responses
- All connected to real LuminalMemory engine (not mocked)

## Known Issues / TODO

### Tool System
- **LLM intent detection is unreliable** — the model sometimes answers instead of routing to a tool, or generates bad search queries. The one-shot example helps but isn't bulletproof. Need better intent classification or a dedicated small model for routing.
- **Tool threshold (0.1) is very low** — almost any query matches "web_search" since its description is broad. Need either: more tools so BM25 has to discriminate, or a confidence check where the LLM confirms it actually needs the tool.
- **Query quality** — first search for "best local LLM models" returned Best Buy results. The model added too many keywords. Need constraints on query length or a rewrite step.
- **No tool result caching** — same question = same search call every time. Should cache recent results.
- **Tool results don't become nodes** — search results aren't stored in the chain, so they can't be recalled later. Should they be?
- **"its 2026 by the way" triggered tool match** — the threshold is too aggressive. Need a way for the LLM to say "no tool needed" even when BM25 matches.

### Persistence
- Active chain resets on page refresh (loads from fixture every time)
- Need IndexedDB persistence for active chain + BM25 index
- Settings (endpoint, model, window size) not saved
  // ... (truncated)
```

</details>

## `agents/reasoning_loop_summary.md`

**1588 lines** | 0 functions/classes

This document serves as a comprehensive technical reference for an advanced, self-correcting AI orchestration system designed to generate highly structured research documents.

**Purpose and Role:**
Its primary purpose is to overcome the inherent limitations of a constrained Language Model (specifically, a 4B parameter model) by acting as a deterministic "system code" layer that drives all content generation. It ensures that the LLM never sees the full document, cannot plan, and only fills one small, atomic block at a time, while maintaining strict adherence to complex structural requirements (like the NASA SBIR format).

**What it Does:**
The system operates through a highly controlled, multi-stage **Orchestration Loop**. This loop manages the entire content lifecycle:

1.  **Configuration and Planning:** It starts with a detailed configuration schema that defines global constraints, universal axioms, and the specific sequence of sections and blocks required for the final document.
2.  **Context Gathering:** For every single block to be written, it executes specialized "Gather Strategies" (e.g., Web Search, Memory Recall, Grep) to collect relevant, structured context data.
3.  **Atomic Generation:** It constructs a highly focused prompt using only the gathered context and calls the LLM for a small, targeted output (an "atomic block").
4.  **Validation and Persistence:** The system rigorously validates the generated content against defined token bounds and structure. Upon success, it saves the content as a persistent **Node** in memory, tagging it with metadata about its source and generation pass.
5.  **Conflict Resolution:** Before final assembly, mandatory post-generation conflict arbitration (Local Impact Analysis and Global Dependency Impact Analyzer) runs to resolve contradictions between different generated sections, ensuring global coherence.

In essence, the document describes a sophisticated meta-engine that manages the entire research process—from initial conceptual constraints through iterative drafting, refinement, and final compilation—making it the core logic layer for generating reliable, structured technical reports.

</details>

<a id="agentswebsearchnotesmd"></a>
<details>
<summary><strong><code>agents/web-search-notes.md</code></strong> — 107 lines, 0 functions</summary>

## `agents/web-search-notes.md`

**107 lines** | 0 functions/classes

This document outlines the technical status, limitations, and future architectural direction for integrating web search capabilities into the project.

### Purpose and Role in the Project
The primary purpose is to enable reliable, deep-dive research by connecting the LLM's ability to generate complex queries with external web content. Its role is defining a critical shift from fully automated, bot-driven searching (which has proven unreliable due to IP blocking) to a **Human-in-the-Loop** collaborative workflow.

### What It Does
1.  **Query Generation:** The system successfully uses an LLM to convert natural language prompts into precise, actionable search queries.
2.  **Current Limitation:** Automated web searching is currently disabled and non-functional because major search engines (Google, DuckDuckGo) actively block automated or headless browser attempts due to IP flagging.
3.  **Proposed Workflow (The Solution):** The ideal design bypasses bot detection entirely by making the user the "human" element:
    *   The AI generates the query.
    *   The system opens a new tab in the **user's actual browser**.
    *   The user performs the search, handles any CAPTCHAs, and clicks the relevant result link.
    *   The user then extracts the content (via URL pasting or a bookmarklet), which is sent back to the system for parsing and storage as indexed research nodes.

### Key Features and Controls
*   **Kill Switch:** A configuration flag (`webSearchEnabled`) controls all search functionality, allowing developers to easily disable the feature without code changes.
*   **Implementation Options:** The design proposes two methods for content extraction: a simple URL paste-back mechanism or a more seamless browser bookmarklet that captures page content directly.

</details>

<details>
<summary><strong><code>data/memory-state.json</code></strong> — 1 lines, 1 functions</summary>

## `data/memory-state.json`

**1 lines** | 1 functions/classes

The source file describes **Luminal Memory**, a foundational, zero-dependency JavaScript library designed to solve the fundamental limitation of Large Language Models (LLMs): context window size.

### Purpose
Its primary purpose is to provide infinite and persistent conversational memory for LLM applications, allowing conversations to continue indefinitely without losing historical context due to token limits or external database dependencies.

### What It Does
Luminal Memory implements a sophisticated, layered memory architecture that manages conversation history through several mechanisms:

1.  **Sliding Window:** Maintains the most recent turns of conversation in active RAM for immediate prompt inclusion.
2.  **Advanced Retrieval:** When context is needed but not in the window, it executes a complex retrieval pipeline using pure mathematical methods (BM25 indexing, Bloom filters, and TF-IDF ranking) to locate and inject highly relevant historical snippets into the current prompt.
3.  **Token Budgeting:** It strictly manages token usage across system prompts, recall context, and the sliding window, ensuring that the final assembled prompt never exceeds the model's maximum capacity.
4.  **Archiving (Cold Storage):** Older conversation history is compressed and archived into IndexedDB storage, keeping the active memory clean while preserving all data for potential future retrieval.
5.  **Tool Management:** It includes a system for discovering and integrating tools based on historical context.

### Role in the Project
The library serves as the central **`LuminalMemory`** class and API entry point. It is highly modular, with dedicated components for every function—including core logic (chaining, window management), search algorithms (`BM25`, `BloomFilter`), storage (`Archive`), and communication (`LLMTransport`). Its role is to orchestrate all these subsystems into a cohesive system that manages the entire lifecycle of conversation data, from real-time chat flow to long-term archival retrieval.

### Exports

- `useLuminal` (function)
- `useLuminal` (function)
- `defaultConfig` (const)
- `Node` (const)
- `Chain` (const)
- `Window` (const)
- `Buffer` (const)
- `SlidingBuffer` (const)
- `RecallBuffer` (const)
- `\r\n         ConversationManager` (const)
- `Compaction` (const)
- `Retrieval` (const)
- `BM25` (const)
- `BloomFilter` (const)
- `\r\n         TfIdf` (const)
- `Archive` (const)
- `MemoryManager` (const)
- `LLMTransport` (const)
- `createConfig` (const)
- `\r\n         defaultConfig` (const)
- `Tool` (const)
- `ToolRegistry` (const)
- `createEphemeralMindTool` (const)
- `\r\n         parseEphemeralPayload` (const)
- `validateTension` (const)
- `Pocket` (const)
- `Settings` (const)

</details>

**Purpose:**
Its primary purpose is to educate developers on every aspect of implementing and managing conversational memory within an LLM application. It guides users from initial setup through advanced architectural patterns.

**What it Does:**
The file simulates a detailed Q&A session that systematically covers the entire lifecycle of using `Luminal Memory`. Key topics covered include:

*   **Setup & Integration:** Providing specific code examples for connecting the library to various LLM backends (Ollama, Llama.cpp, LM Studio, and custom OpenAI APIs).
*   **Core Concepts:** Explaining fundamental memory structures like "Nodes," the linear conversation history ("Chain"), and how context is managed using a limited "Sliding Window."
*   **Advanced Features:** Detailing complex mechanisms such as various retrieval pipelines (BM25, Bloom), managing memory size through trimming/compaction, and building custom tool systems.
*   **Practical Concerns:** Addressing configuration options, state persistence (saving/loading memory), integration examples (React, CLI), performance limits, and troubleshooting FAQs.

**Role in the Project:**
This fixture acts as a single, highly detailed educational resource. It allows developers to understand not only *how* to use the library but also *why* certain architectural decisions are necessary for building robust, scalable, and context-aware AI applications.

## `demo/ui/css/internal/atoms/reset.css`

**73 lines** | 0 functions/classes

This file serves as the global CSS reset and base styling layer for the entire application. Its primary purpose is to establish a consistent, predictable starting point for all elements, ensuring visual uniformity regardless of default browser styles.

**What it does:**
It performs several key functions:
1.  **Resets Defaults:** It eliminates default margins, paddings, and box-sizing inconsistencies across all HTML elements (`*`, `::before`, `::after`).
2.  **Sets Global Typography & Layout:** It defines the base styles for the `body` (including background color, primary text color, font family, and line height) using CSS variables.
3.  **Neutralizes Elements:** It resets common interactive elements like buttons, inputs, textareas, links, and lists to remove default borders, outlines, and list markers, allowing them to inherit styles cleanly.
4.  **Styles Scrollbars:** It provides custom styling for the browser's scrollbar appearance.

**Role in the Project:**
This file acts as the foundational layer of the project's CSS architecture. By handling all global resets and defining core visual variables (colors, fonts), it ensures that subsequent components and modules can build upon a clean slate without conflicting with unpredictable browser defaults.

</details>

This CSS file styles two distinct, non-core user interface components intended to display supplementary information in the whitespace flanking the main chat area: **Sidebar Statistics** and a **Pocket Queue**.

**Purpose and Role:**
The primary role of this code is to provide structured, visually themed readouts (styled like sci-fi terminal data) for secondary content. These elements are designed to enhance the application's aesthetic by utilizing dedicated side areas without interfering with the main chat flow.

**Functionality:**

1.  **Sidebar Stats (`.sidebar-stats`):**

    *   It is fixed to the left side of the screen and uses monospace fonts for a technical, data-readout appearance.
    *   The styling includes specific classes to differentiate between general values, secondary values, and highlighted (accent) values.
    *   **Responsiveness:** It is hidden entirely on screens narrower than 1100px.

2.  **Pocket Queue (`.pocket-queue`):**
    *   This component manages a list of items or tasks ("pockets") displayed in the right whitespace.
    *   It uses a fixed position and features an initial opacity of zero, requiring an active class (`--active`) to become visible (allowing for smooth transitions).
    *   Each item is styled with an accent border and background, giving it a distinct "card" appearance.
    *   **Functionality:** It includes specific styling for items that have been consumed or completed, visually fading them out and applying a strikethrough effect.
    *   **Responsiveness:** Like the stats, it is hidden on screens narrower than 1100px.

It dictates a strict load order—which is crucial because many scripts operate as non-module globals and rely on components loaded previously. By listing all modules (from basic components like `modal` to complex controllers like `app.js`), it ensures that the entire UI—including background animations, chat rendering, tool execution chains, and settings panels—is loaded correctly and in the proper sequence before the main application controller can boot up and wire all event handlers together.

## `demo/ui/js/internal/app.js`

**535 lines** | 13 functions/classes

This file serves as the **main application controller** for Luminal Memory, acting as the primary initialization point that sets up the entire user interface and core logic.

**Purpose:**
Its purpose is to bootstrap the application by configuring global settings, managing persistent state, initializing all necessary UI components, and establishing the communication flow between these elements and the underlying memory engine.

**What It Does:**
1.  **Configuration & State Management:** Defines a comprehensive `CONFIG` object (containing API endpoints, model names, and various operational thresholds). It initializes the core `LuminalMemory` instance and manages the application state by providing functions to automatically save (`autoSave`) and restore (`tryRestore`) the conversation history to a server.
2.  **Component Initialization:** Calls initialization routines for all major UI modules (e.g., `Topbar`, `Chat`, `Modal`, `Input`), ensuring they are ready to receive data and handle user interactions.
3.  **Conversation Flow Handling:** It manages the loading of conversation history, first attempting to restore a saved state; if restoration fails, it loads the initial "fixture" data. It then renders this full history into the chat interface.
4.  **Tool Integration:** It registers specialized tools (like Ephemeral Mind and Web Search) with the memory system, making them available for use by the AI model.

**Role in the Project:**
The file acts as the central orchestrator. It does not contain heavy logic itself; rather, it delegates complex tasks—such as core reasoning loops (`sovereign-loop.js`) and mock data handling (`mock-responses.js`)—to other modules while ensuring that all components are correctly wired together and that the application state is persistent across sessions.

### Functions & Methods

#### `autoSave()` — function, line 43

* **Purpose:** Asynchronously exports the current application memory state and saves it to a remote server endpoint (`/api/state/save`).
* **Inputs:** None
* **Outputs:** Promise<void> - Indicates completion of the save operation (or failure, which is caught internally).
* **Behavioral Notes:** The function uses `try...catch` to gracefully handle any failures during state export or network transmission, logging a warning instead of crashing. It relies on external objects (`memory`) and the global `fetch` API for functionality.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  async function autoSave() {
    try {
      var state = await memory.export();
      // Save to server (JSON file on disk)
      await fetch('/api/state/save', {
        method: 'POST',
        headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
        body: JSON.stringify(state)
      });
      console.log('[AutoSave] Saved (' + memory.chain.length + ' nodes)');
    } catch (e) {
      console.warn('[AutoSave] Failed:', e.message);
    }
  }
```

</details>

#### `tryRestore()` — function, line 58

* **Purpose:** Attempts to load and restore application state, specifically chain node data, from a remote API endpoint.
* **Inputs:** None
* **Outputs:** Promise<boolean> - Returns `true` if the state was successfully loaded and imported, otherwise returns `false`.
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * It makes an asynchronous GET request to `/api/state/load`.
    * If the response is not OK or the received state data is invalid (missing chain structure or nodes), it immediately returns `false`.
    * Before importing, it explicitly deletes the `config` property from the loaded state object to prevent overwriting current configuration settings.
    * It relies on an external module/object named `memory` which must have an asynchronous `import(state)` method.
    * If any network or processing error occurs, it logs a warning and returns `false`.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  async function tryRestore() {
    try {
      var res = await fetch('/api/state/load');
      if (!res.ok) return false;
      var state = await res.json();
      if (!state || !state.chain || !state.chain.nodes || state.chain.nodes.length === 0) return false;
      // Import chain data only — don't overwrite current config
      delete state.config;
      await memory.import(state);
      console.log('[Restore] Restored ' + memory.chain.length + ' nodes from server');
      return true;
    } catch (e) {
      console.warn('[Restore] Failed:', e.message);
      return false;
    }
  }
```

</details>

#### `updatePocketQueueDisplay()` — function, line 76

* **Purpose:** Updates the visual display of the pending items queue within the DOM element with ID 'pocket-queue'.
* **Inputs:** None
* **Outputs:** None
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * The function clears all existing children from the `#pocket-queue` container before redrawing.
    * It reads the current list of queued items from `memory.pocket.queue`.
    * If the queue is empty, it removes the 'pocket-queue--active' class and exits.
    * If items are present, it adds the 'pocket-queue--active' class and creates a new display item for each element in the queue.
    * Each displayed item shows the item ID and a truncated version (max 50 characters) of the content.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function updatePocketQueueDisplay() {
    var queueEl = document.getElementById('pocket-queue');
    if (!queueEl) return;
    var items = queueEl.querySelectorAll('.pocket-queue__item');
    items.forEach(function (el) { el.remove(); });

    var pending = memory.pocket.queue;
    if (pending.length === 0) {
      queueEl.classList.remove('pocket-queue--active');
      return;
    }

    queueEl.classList.add('pocket-queue--active');
    pending.forEach(function (n) {
      var item = document.createElement('div');
      item.className = 'pocket-queue__item';
      item.textContent = '#' + n.id + ' ' + n.content.slice(0, 50) + (n.content.length > 50 ? '...' : '');
      queueEl.appendChild(item);
    });
  }
```

</details>

#### `init()` — function, line 105

* **Purpose:** Initializes the entire application state and UI components by setting up topbar, chat interface, modal, memory system, and loading conversation history either from a fixture or restored saved data.
* **Inputs:** None (Relies on global scope variables like `CONFIG`, `conversation`, `memory`, etc.)
* **Outputs:** None
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * Calls multiple component initialization methods (`Topbar.init()`, `Chat.init()`, `Modal.init()`).
    * Asynchronously initializes the memory system (`await memory.init()`).
    * Determines if state needs restoration by calling `tryRestore()`:
        * **If `restored` is false (Fresh Start):** It processes the `conversation` fixture, appending turns to `memory.chain` and adding nodes to `memory.bm25`. It then renders messages from the beginning of the conversation up to the window size limit.
        * **If `restored` is true (Restoring State):** It uses all nodes from the restored chain (`memory.chain.all()`) to render a full, scrollable history, starting at the appropriate window boundary defined by `CONFIG.windowSize`.
    * The function performs significant side effects by manipulating global UI components and internal memory state.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  async function init() {
    Topbar.init();
    Topbar.setConfig(CONFIG);
    Chat.init();
    Modal.init();

    await memory.init();

    // Try restoring saved state — skip fixture if we have saved data
    var restored = await tryRestore();

    if (!restored) {
      // Load fixture into chain (fresh start)
      for (var i = 0; i < conversation.length - 1; i += 2) {
        var user = conversation[i];
        var assistant = conversation[i + 1];
        if (user && assistant) {
          memory.chain.appendTurn(user.content, assistant.content);
          var node = memory.chain.all()[memory.chain.length - 1];
          memory.bm25.add(node);
        }
      }
    }

    // Render preloaded messages (from fixture or restored chain)
    var allNodes = memory.chain.all();
    if (!restored) {
      var windowStart = conversation.length - (CONFIG.windowSize * 2);
      for (var j = 0; j < conversation.length; j++) {
        if (j === windowStart) Chat.renderWindowBoundary();
        Chat.renderMessage(conversation[j].role, conversation[j].content, Math.ceil((j + 1) / 2), false);
      }
    } else {
      // Render ALL nodes from restored chain (full scrollable history)
      var allRestoredNodes = memory.chain.all();
      var windowStart = Math.max(0, allRestoredNodes.length - CONFIG.windowSize);
      for (var k = 0; k < allRestoredNodes.length; k++) {
        if (k === windowStart) Chat.renderWindowBoundary();
```

#### `handleSend(msg)` — function, line 317

* **Purpose:** Processes a user message by updating memory, determining if a specialized chain or general tool is required, executing the appropriate logic, and displaying results in the chat interface.
* **Inputs:**
    * `msg` (`string`): The text message sent by the user.
* **Outputs:** None (The function performs side effects on the UI, memory state, and chat history).
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * The function immediately disables the input field and updates the top bar status upon execution.
    * It first attempts to match the message against a predefined `ToolChain`. If successful, it executes the chain logic.
    * If no chain is matched, it attempts to retrieve and execute the best matching tool from `memory.toolRegistry`.
    * All interactions (user messages, system actions, tool results) are rendered into the chat history (`Chat.renderMessage`, `Chat.renderSystem`).
    * The user message is always appended to the persistent memory chain (`memory.chain`) and indexed for retrieval (`memory.bm25.add`).
    * If a web search tool is used, the function extracts content from the results and stores them as research nodes in both the chat history and the BM25 index, making them searchable later.
    * The entire process is asynchronous, involving multiple `await` calls for tool execution.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  async function handleSend(msg) {
    if (!ready) return;

    Input.disable();
    Topbar.setStatus('active', 'SOVEREIGN MIND');
    Chat.renderMessage('user', msg, memory.chain.length + 1, true);

    try {
      var pendingNode = memory.chain.append('user', msg);
      memory.bm25.add(pendingNode);

      // BM25-discovered tools
      var toolResults = [];
      var chainMatch = ToolChain.match(msg);

      if (chainMatch) {
        Topbar.setStatus('active', 'CHAIN: ' + chainMatch.recipe.name.toUpperCase());
        Chat.renderSystem('\u2692 Running chain: ' + chainMatch.recipe.name);
        var chainResult = await ToolChain.execute(chainMatch.recipe, msg);
        if (chainResult && chainResult.formatted) {
          toolResults.push({ name: chainMatch.recipe.name, result: { formatted: chainResult.formatted } });
          Chat.renderSystem('\u2713 Chain complete: ' + chainResult.steps.length + ' steps');
        }
      } else {
        var matchedTools = memory.toolRegistry.retrieve(msg);
        if (matchedTools.length > 0 && matchedTools[0].score > 0.5) {
          var topTool = matchedTools[0].tool;
          Topbar.setStatus('active', 'TOOL: ' + topTool.name.toUpperCase());
          Chat.renderSystem('\u2692 Using tool: ' + topTool.name);
          var toolResult = await memory.toolRegistry.execute(topTool.name, { query: msg }, { query: msg, chain: memory.chain, config: memory.config });
          if (toolResult.success) {
            toolResults.push({ name: topTool.name, result: toolResult.result });
            Chat.renderSystem('\u2713 ' + topTool.name + ' (' + toolResult.elapsed + 'ms)');

            // Store web search results as research nodes (BM25 indexed, persistent)
            if (topTool.name === 'web_search' && toolResult.result.results) {
              toolResult.result.results.forEach(function (r) {
                if (r.content && r.content.length > 30) {
                  var researchNode = memory.chain.append('system', '[Research: ' + (r.title || 'Web') + '] ' + r.content.slice(0, 800));
                  memory.bm25.add(researchNode);
  // ... (truncated)
```

</details>

#### `handleTrim()` — function, line 465

* **Purpose:** Initiates a trimming process, archiving nodes outside of a specified keep range and updating the UI with the results.
* **Inputs:** None (Relies on global state: `ready`, `SelectionMode`, `memory`, `Chat`).
* **Outputs:** None
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * The function first checks if the application is ready (`!ready`) and exits if not.
    * If a selection mode is active, it cancels it before starting the trim process.
    * It uses an asynchronous callback provided by `SelectionMode.startTrim()`, which receives data containing the start and end IDs of the nodes to keep (`data`).
    * Upon successful trimming:
        1.  It calls `memory.trimKeepRange` using the provided IDs, receiving a result object detailing archived nodes before and after the range.
        2.  It calculates the total number of archived nodes.
        3.  It removes the archival markers from the chat history (`Chat.removeArchived`).
        4.  It displays a success message in the system chat.
    * If any step fails, it catches the error and displays a failure message in the system chat.
    * Finally, it calls `refreshStats()` regardless of success or failure to update statistics.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function handleTrim() {
    if (!ready) return;
    if (SelectionMode.isActive()) { SelectionMode.cancel(); return; }
    SelectionMode.startTrim(async function (data) {
      try {
        var result = await memory.trimKeepRange({ keepStart: data.keepStartId, keepEnd: data.keepEndId });
        var archived = 0;
        if (result.before) archived += result.before.metadata.nodeCount;
        if (result.after) archived += result.after.metadata.nodeCount;
        Chat.removeArchived({ keepStart: data.keepStartId, keepEnd: data.keepEndId });
        Chat.renderSystem('\u2702\uFE0F Trimmed ' + archived + ' nodes to archive.');
      } catch (err) {
        Chat.renderSystem('\u274C Trim failed: ' + err.message);
      }
      refreshStats();
    });
  }
```

</details>

#### `handleBranch()` — function, line 484

* **Purpose:** Initiates the process of creating a branch from a selected node, archiving related chat history and updating system statistics upon completion.
* **Inputs:** None (Relies on global state: `ready`, `SelectionMode`, `memory`, `Chat`).
* **Outputs:** None
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * The function immediately exits if the application is not marked as ready (`!ready`).
    * If an active selection mode exists, it cancels that mode before starting the branch process.
    * The core logic executes asynchronously within a callback provided to `SelectionMode.startBranch()`, which receives data containing the source node ID (`data.fromNodeId`).
    * It attempts to retrieve the branch from memory using `memory.branchFrom(data.fromNodeId)`.
    * If successful, it removes archived chat history and displays a system message detailing the number of nodes archived.
    * If no nodes are found for archiving, it displays an appropriate system message.
    * It uses a try/catch block to handle potential errors during branching, displaying the error message in the chat interface.
    * The function always calls `refreshStats()` regardless of success or failure.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function handleBranch() {
    if (!ready) return;
    if (SelectionMode.isActive()) { SelectionMode.cancel(); return; }
    SelectionMode.startBranch(async function (data) {
      try {
        var result = await memory.branchFrom(data.fromNodeId);
        if (result) {
          Chat.removeArchived({ fromNodeId: data.fromNodeId });
          Chat.renderSystem('\u2325 Branched from Node ' + data.fromNodeId + '. ' + result.metadata.nodeCount + ' nodes archived.');
        } else {
          Chat.renderSystem('Nothing to archive before Node ' + data.fromNodeId + '.');
        }
      } catch (err) {
        Chat.renderSystem('\u274C Branch failed: ' + err.message);
      }
      refreshStats();
    });
  }
```

</details>

#### `refreshStats()` — function, line 504

* **Purpose:** Updates various UI elements, including the top bar and side archives section, with current application statistics retrieved from memory status.
* **Inputs:** None
* **Outputs:** None
* **Behavioral Notes:** Calls `memory.status()` to retrieve a comprehensive status object (`s`), which is then used to update `Topbar` and set the text content of the DOM element with the ID 'side-archives'. If the side archives element does not exist, no error occurs.

<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function refreshStats() {
    var s = memory.status();
    Topbar.updateStats(s.totalNodes, memory.getWindow().length, s.memoryUsageMB, s.totalTokens);
    var sideArchives = document.getElementById('side-archives');
    if (sideArchives) sideArchives.textContent = s.archiveBlocks;
  }
```

#### `recheckLLM()` — function, line 514

* **Purpose:** Checks the connectivity status of the configured Large Language Model (LLM) endpoint and updates the UI accordingly.
* **Inputs:** None
* **Outputs:** void
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * This function uses an `AbortController` (`_recheckAbort`) to cancel any previously running LLM connection checks before starting a new one, preventing race conditions.
    * It constructs the API check URL dynamically based on `memory.config.apiFormat` ('ollama' vs. other formats).
    * If the fetch request fails due to an `AbortError`, it silently returns; otherwise, any network error sets `llmAvailable` to `false`.
    * The function has side effects: it updates the global state variable `llmAvailable`, calls `Topbar.setStatus()` to update the UI status indicator, and potentially calls `Chat.renderSystem()` to display a connection message if successful.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  async function recheckLLM() {
    if (_recheckAbort) _recheckAbort.abort();
    _recheckAbort = new AbortController();
    var endpoint = memory.config.endpoint;
    var apiFormat = memory.config.apiFormat;
    try {
      var checkUrl = apiFormat === 'ollama' ? endpoint + '/api/tags' : endpoint + '/v1/models';
      var res = await fetch(checkUrl, { signal: _recheckAbort.signal });
      llmAvailable = res.ok;
    } catch (e) {
      if (e.name === 'AbortError') return;
      llmAvailable = false;
    }
    _recheckAbort = null;
    Topbar.setStatus('active', llmAvailable ? 'READY' : 'MOCK LLM');
    if (llmAvailable) {
      Chat.renderSystem('\u2713 Connected to ' + apiFormat + ' at ' + endpoint + (memory.config.model ? ' (' + memory.config.model + ')' : ''));
    }
  }
```

</details>

</details>

## `demo/ui/js/internal/canvas.js`

**245 lines** | 10 functions/classes

This module manages the entire visual background environment for the application. Its primary purpose is to create a dynamic, animated canvas featuring a combination of data visualization (ASCII art) and abstract motion graphics (morphing shapes).

**What it Does:**
1.  **GIF Processing:** It loads an external GIF file using `gifuct-js`, decoding it frame by frame.
2.  **Mask Generation:** Instead of displaying the GIF directly, it samples each frame's brightness intensity to create a low-resolution "mask." This mask determines where content should appear.
3.  **ASCII Rendering:** It maps the brightness values from the mask onto a grid across the main canvas. Brighter areas correspond to specific ASCII characters (e.g., `.` through `@`), creating a dynamic, text-based visualization that reacts to the GIF's movement.
4.  **Shape Animation:** It renders several abstract, morphing shapes that continuously change their number of sides and drift across the screen, adding an element of organic motion.
5.  **Interactivity:** It exposes a `pulse()` method, allowing external components to trigger a visual "recall" or pulse effect across both the ASCII characters and the animated shapes.

**Role in the Project:**
The module serves as the primary, continuously running background layer. It provides the aesthetic context for the application, ensuring that the user interface is visually engaging and reactive by tying its core elements (ASCII density and shape movement) to a looping, time-based animation source (the GIF).

### Functions & Methods

#### `loadGif()` — function, line 48

* **Purpose:** Asynchronously fetches, parses, and decompresses a GIF file to prepare it for display on a canvas element.
* **Inputs:** None
* **Outputs:** None
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * This function performs side effects by modifying global state variables (`gifCanvas`, `frames`, `gifReady`).
    * It depends on the external library `gifuct` for parsing and decompression.
    * Upon successful loading, it sets the dimensions of `gifCanvas` using the dimensions of the first frame and sets `gifReady` to `true`.
    * If the GIF fails to load or process, a warning is logged to the console, but execution continues without erroring out.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function loadGif() {
    fetch('ui/assets/bg.gif')
      .then(function (resp) { return resp.arrayBuffer(); })
      .then(function (buff) {
        var gif = gifuct.parseGIF(buff);
        frames = gifuct.decompressFrames(gif, true);
        if (frames.length > 0) {
          gifCanvas.width = frames[0].dims.width;
          gifCanvas.height = frames[0].dims.height;
          gifReady = true;
          console.log('[Canvas] GIF loaded: ' + frames.length + ' frames');
        }
      })
      .catch(function (err) {
        console.warn('[Canvas] Failed to load gif:', err);
      });
  }
```

</details>

#### `resize()` — function, line 66

* **Purpose:** Resizes the main canvas and mask canvas to match the current window dimensions and recalculates internal grid parameters for shape initialization.
* **Inputs:** None
* **Outputs:** void
* **Behavioral Notes:** This function modifies several global variables (`width`, `height`, `cols`, `rows`) and updates the dimensions of both the main canvas and the mask canvas. It concludes by calling `initShapes()`, which is responsible for setting up the visual elements based on the new dimensions.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function resize() {
    width = canvas.width = window.innerWidth;
    height = canvas.height = window.innerHeight;
    maskCanvas.width = Math.ceil(width / 4);
    maskCanvas.height = Math.ceil(height / 4);
    cols = Math.ceil(width / stepX);
    rows = Math.ceil(height / stepY);
    initShapes();
  }
```

</details>

#### `updateMask(timestamp)` — function, line 77

* **Purpose:** Updates the mask canvas by advancing through GIF frames based on elapsed time and drawing the resulting frame data onto a scaled output image.
* **Inputs:**
    * `timestamp` (number): The current high-resolution timestamp used to determine if enough time has passed to advance to the next GIF frame.
* **Outputs:** Uint8ClampedArray: Raw pixel data representing the mask for the current frame, scaled to the dimensions of the mask canvas.
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * This function relies heavily on external state variables (`gifReady`, `frames`, `lastFrameTime`, `frameIdx`, `gifCtx`, `maskCtx`, etc.) which manage the GIF playback state and drawing contexts.
    * If the GIF is not ready or has no frames, it immediately returns `null`.
    * It advances the frame index only if the time elapsed since `lastFrameTime` exceeds `frameDelay`.
    * The function performs multiple side effects by drawing patches onto internal canvas contexts (`gifCtx`, `maskCtx`).

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function updateMask(timestamp) {
    if (!gifReady || frames.length === 0) return null;

    // Advance frame based on delay
    if (timestamp - lastFrameTime > frameDelay) {
      frameIdx = (frameIdx + 1) % frames.length;
      lastFrameTime = timestamp;
      frameDelay = frames[frameIdx].delay || 100;

      // Draw the frame patch onto the gif canvas
      var frame = frames[frameIdx];
      var dims = frame.dims;
      var imageData = new ImageData(frame.patch, dims.width, dims.height);

      // Handle disposal (simplified: just draw over)
      if (frame.disposalType === 2) {
        gifCtx.clearRect(0, 0, gifCanvas.width, gifCanvas.height);
      }
      
      // Create temp canvas for this frame's patch
      var tempCanvas = document.createElement('canvas');
      tempCanvas.width = dims.width;
      tempCanvas.height = dims.height;
      var tempCtx = tempCanvas.getContext('2d');
      tempCtx.putImageData(imageData, 0, 0);
      
      gifCtx.drawImage(tempCanvas, dims.left, dims.top);
    }

    // Scale gif frame down to mask canvas
    maskCtx.drawImage(gifCanvas, 0, 0, maskCanvas.width, maskCanvas.height);
    return maskCtx.getImageData(0, 0, maskCanvas.width, maskCanvas.height).data;
  }
```

</details>

#### `drawAscii(maskData)` — function, line 112

* **Purpose:** Draws an ASCII art representation onto the canvas based on color data provided in `maskData`, where character selection and visibility are determined by pixel brightness.
* **Inputs:**
    * `maskData` (`Array<number>`): A flattened array containing RGBA color values (R, G, B, A) for the mask image used to determine character density and type.
* **Outputs:** None (Side effect: Draws text characters onto the canvas context).
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * The function relies on several external variables/context objects (`ctx`, `maskCanvas`, `width`, `height`, `rows`, `cols`, `stepX`, `stepY`, `asciiChars`, `GREY`) which must be defined and accessible in the scope.
    * It calculates brightness using a standard weighted average (luminosity formula).
    * If the calculated brightness is very low (`< 0.1`), no character is drawn for that point, effectively skipping dark areas.
    * The ASCII character displayed is determined by mapping the normalized brightness value to an index within `asciiChars`.
    * It draws characters using a semi-transparent grey fill color whose alpha channel is proportional to the calculated brightness.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function drawAscii(maskData) {
    if (!maskData) return;

    ctx.font = '11px "SF Mono", Consolas, monospace';

    var mw = maskCanvas.width;
    var scaleX = mw / width;
    var scaleY = maskCanvas.height / height;

    for (var r = 0; r < rows; r++) {
      for (var c = 0; c < cols; c++) {
        var x = c * stepX;
        var y = r * stepY;

        var mx = Math.floor(x * scaleX);
        var my = Math.floor(y * scaleY);
        var idx = (my * mw + mx) * 4;

        var red = maskData[idx];
        var green = maskData[idx + 1];
        var blue = maskData[idx + 2];
        var brightness = (red * 0.299 + green * 0.587 + blue * 0.114) / 255;

        if (brightness < 0.1) continue;

        var charIdx = Math.floor(brightness * (asciiChars.length - 1));
        var char = asciiChars[Math.min(charIdx, asciiChars.length - 1)];
        if (char === ' ') continue;

        var alpha = brightness * 0.35;
        ctx.fillStyle = 'rgba(' + GREY.r + ',' + GREY.g + ',' + GREY.b + ',' + alpha + ')';
        ctx.fillText(char, x, y);
      }
    }
  }
```

</details>

#### `initShapes()` — function, line 149

* **Purpose:** Initializes the global `shapes` array by creating a specified number of random shape objects with various physical and animation properties.
* **Inputs:** None (Relies on global variables: `SHAPE_COUNT`, `width`, and `height`).
* **Outputs:** None (Mutates the global variable `shapes`).
* **Behavioral Notes:** Resets the global `shapes` array before populating it. Each shape object is given randomized starting positions, sizes, side counts, animation speeds, and opacities, preparing them for subsequent simulation or drawing cycles.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function initShapes() {
    shapes = [];
    for (var i = 0; i < SHAPE_COUNT; i++) {
      shapes.push({
        x: width * 0.15 + Math.random() * width * 0.7,
        y: height * 0.15 + Math.random() * height * 0.7,
        size: 25 + Math.random() * 40,
        currentSides: 3 + Math.floor(Math.random() * 5),
        targetSides: 3 + Math.floor(Math.random() * 5),
        morphProgress: 0,
        morphSpeed: 0.002 + Math.random() * 0.002,
        rotation: Math.random() * Math.PI * 2,
        rotSpeed: (Math.random() - 0.5) * 0.0015,
        driftX: (Math.random() - 0.5) * 0.1,
        driftY: (Math.random() - 0.5) * 0.06,
        opacity: 0.05 + Math.random() * 0.04
      });
    }
  }
```

</details>

#### `drawMorphingShape(s)` — function, line 171

* **Purpose:** Draws a continuously morphing shape on the canvas by interpolating between two sets of vertices while updating its position and rotation.
* **Inputs:** s (Object): The state object containing parameters for the shape's drawing and animation, including `morphSpeed`, `currentSides`, `targetSides`, `morphProgress`, `x`, `y`, `rotation`, `size`, `opacity`, `driftX`, `driftY`, `rotSpeed`.
* **Outputs:** None (Draws directly to the canvas context).
* **Behavioral Notes:**
    * **State Mutation:** This function heavily mutates the input object `s` by updating `s.morphProgress`, `s.currentSides`, `s.targetSides`, `s.x`, `s.y`, and `s.rotation`.
    * **Morphing Logic:** The shape morphs using an easing function (`ease`) that interpolates coordinates between the vertices defined by `s.currentSides` and `s.targetSides` based on `s.morphProgress`.
    * **Cycle Reset:** When `s.morphProgress` reaches 1, it resets to 0, updates `s.currentSides`, and randomly selects a new `s.targetSides` (between 3 and 8).
    * **Boundary Wrapping:** The shape's position (`x`, `y`) wraps around the canvas boundaries defined by `width` and `height`.
    * **Dependencies:** Relies on external variables/functions: `pulseIntensity`, `ctx` (canvas context), `GREY` (color object), `width`, `height`, and a linear interpolation function `lerp()`.

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function drawMorphingShape(s) {
    var speed = s.morphSpeed;
    if (pulseIntensity > 0) speed *= 1 + pulseIntensity * 3;
    s.morphProgress += speed;

    if (s.morphProgress >= 1) {
      s.morphProgress = 0;
      s.currentSides = s.targetSides;
      s.targetSides = 3 + Math.floor(Math.random() * 6);
    }

    s.x += s.driftX;
    s.y += s.driftY;
    s.rotation += s.rotSpeed;

    if (s.x < -s.size * 2) s.x = width + s.size;
    if (s.x > width + s.size * 2) s.x = -s.size;
    if (s.y < -s.size * 2) s.y = height + s.size;
    if (s.y > height + s.size * 2) s.y = -s.size;

    var maxVerts = Math.max(s.currentSides, s.targetSides);
    var p = s.morphProgress;
    var ease = p < 0.5 ? 2 * p * p : 1 - Math.pow(-2 * p + 2, 2) / 2;

    ctx.beginPath();
    for (var i = 0; i < maxVerts; i++) {
      var a1 = (i / s.currentSides) * Math.PI * 2 + s.rotation;
      var a2 = (i / s.targetSides) * Math.PI * 2 + s.rotation;
      var px = lerp(s.x + Math.cos(a1) * s.size, s.x + Math.cos(a2) * s.size, ease);
      var py = lerp(s.y + Math.sin(a1) * s.size, s.y + Math.sin(a2) * s.size, ease);
      if (i === 0) ctx.moveTo(px, py); else ctx.lineTo(px, py);
    }
    ctx.closePath();

    ctx.strokeStyle = 'rgba(' + GREY.r + ',' + GREY.g + ',' + GREY.b + ',' + s.opacity + ')';
    ctx.lineWidth = 1;
    ctx.stroke();
  }
```

</details>

* **Purpose:** Iterates through a collection of stored shapes and calls a dedicated function to draw each shape sequentially.
* **Inputs:** None

```javascript
    for (var i = 0; i < shapes.length; i++) {
      drawMorphingShape(shapes[i]);
    }
  }
```

</details>

#### `animate(timestamp)` — function, line 217

* **Purpose:** Executes the main animation loop, clearing the canvas and redrawing all visual elements (mask, ASCII art, shapes) based on elapsed time until the next frame is requested.
* **Inputs:** `timestamp` (number): The high-resolution timestamp provided by the browser indicating when the current frame should be drawn.
* **Outputs:** None (It triggers a continuous animation loop via `requestAnimationFrame`).
* **Behavioral Notes:** This function must be called recursively using `requestAnimationFrame(animate)` to maintain the animation cycle; otherwise, the drawing will stop. It modifies global state variables (`time`, `pulseIntensity`) and draws directly onto the canvas context (`ctx`).

<details>
<summary>Source</summary>

```javascript
  function animate(timestamp) {
    time = timestamp;
    ctx.clearRect(0, 0, width, height);

    if (pulseIntensity > 0) {
      pulseIntensity -= 0.008;
      if (pulseIntensity < 0) pulseIntensity = 0;
    }

    var maskData = updateMask(timestamp);
    drawAscii(maskData);
    drawShapes();

    requestAnimationFrame(animate);
  }
```

</details>

```javascript
    pulseIntensity = 1.0;
  }
```

</details>
